title: "Entropy & Information Theory"
question: "A language model&#39;s validation loss is 2.0 nats per token. What is its perplexity, and how many bits per token is that?"
answer: "Perplexity <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.3669em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">e</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">2.0</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">7.39</span></span></span></span>: on average as uncertain as a uniform choice among about 7.4 tokens. In bits, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2.0/</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2.89</span></span></span></span> bits per token. Comparing losses across models with different tokenisers needs bits per byte or per character, not per token."

Do the strategic practice problems"}, {"title": "Mathematics for Machine Learning, ch. 6 and 8.3 (MLE)", "url": "https://mml-book.github.io/", "note": ""}, {"title": "John Schulman, Approximating KL Divergence", "url": "http://joschu.net/blog/kl-approx.html", "note": "The k1/k2/k3 estimators used in PPO and GRPO"}, {"title": "Fanout 01.14 Moving Averages (free)", "url": "https://fanout.sh/ai/lessons/math-fundamentals-moving-averages", "note": "Short; needed for Adam and EMA in 0.5"}]
html: "<section class=\"art\"><h2>Entropy: measuring surprise in bits</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Shannon, &quot;A Mathematical Theory of Communication&quot; (1948) · Cover and Thomas, Elements of Information Theory ch. 2 · Goodfellow et al., Deep Learning §3.13 · Fanout 01.11</p><h3>Why this matters</h3>\n<p>Every language model is trained by minimising <strong>cross-entropy</strong>. When a paper reports &quot;perplexity&quot; or &quot;bits per byte&quot;, that is entropy too. This lesson builds the idea from one question: how surprised should you be?</p>\n<h3>The idea in plain words</h3>\n<p>If something certain happens, you learn nothing. If something rare happens, you learn a lot. Information theory turns that feeling into a number, the <strong>surprise</strong> of an outcome with probability <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span>:</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord text\"><span class=\"mord\">surprise</span></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span A 99% coin has only 0.081 bits. Entropy is highest when every outcome is equally likely.</p>\n<h3>Worked example 2: four outcomes</h3>\n<p>Weather is sunny 50%, cloudy 25%, rainy 12.5%, snowy 12.5%.</p>\n<div bits</strong></td>\n</tr>\n</tbody></table></div>\n<p>If all four were equally likely, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0813em;\">H</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2</span></span></span></span> bits, the maximum for four outcomes (<span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.9386em;vertical-align:-0.2441em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\"><span class=\"mop\">lo<span style=\"margin-right:0.0139em;\">g</span></span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.207em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.4559em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">2</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.2441em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">4</span></span></span></span>).</p>\n<p>This number has a physical meaning. If you sent the weather each day as a binary code, the best possible code (e.g. sunny = <code>0</code>, cloudy = <code>10</code>, rainy = <code>110</code>, snowy = <code>111</code>) uses 1.75 bits per day on average. No code can do better. That is Shannon&#39;s theorem.</p>\n<figure class=\"dg viz\" data-viz=\"entropy\" data-p=\"{&quot;names&quot;:[&quot;sunny&quot;,&quot;cloudy&quot;,&quot;rainy&quot;,&quot;snowy&quot;],&quot;w&quot;:[4,2,1,1]}\"><div class=\"vz\"></div><figcaption>Drag each bar. The label shows its probability and its surprise. Push one bar to the top and entropy falls towards 0. Press &quot;uniform&quot; for the maximum, 2 bits.</figcaption></figure>\n<h3>Bits or nats?</h3>\n<p><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.9386em;vertical-align:-0.2441em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\"><span class=\"mop\">lo<span style=\"margin-right:0.0139em;\">g</span></span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.207em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.4559em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">2</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.2441em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span> gives <strong>bits</strong>. The natural log <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span></span></span></span> gives <strong>nats</strong>. PyTorch uses <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span></span></span></span>, so its losses are in nats. To convert, 1 nat <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.3669em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1/</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">2</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1.443</span></span></span></span> bits.</p>\n<h3>Worked example 3: cross-entropy, the loss you will use</h3>\n<p>Your model looks at a photo and predicts cat 0.7, dog 0.2, bird 0.1. The true answer is cat.</p>\n<p><strong>Cross-entropy</strong> measures the average surprise <em>when you use the model&#39;s probabilities <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> but reality follows <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span></em>:</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0813em;\">H</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mpunct\">,</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:2.3em;vertical-align:-1.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mop op-limits\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:1.05em;\"><span style=\"top:-1.9em;margin-left:0em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mord\">.</span></span></span></span></span></div>\n<p>The true label is one-hot (cat = 1, the rest 0), so only the cat term survives:</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord text\"><span class=\"mord\">loss</span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7778em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0.7</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0.357</span><span class=\"mord text\"><span class=\"mord\"> nats</span></span><span class=\"mord\">.</span></span></span></span></span></div>\n<ul>\n<li>If the model said cat 0.99, the loss is 0.010.</li>\n<li>If it said cat 0.1, the loss is 2.303.</li>\n<li>If it said cat 0.0, the loss is infinite. Confident and wrong is punished hardest.</li>\n</ul>\n<p>For a language model, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span> is &quot;the actual next token&quot; and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> is the model&#39;s softmax over the vocabulary. Training lowers the average surprise at each real next token.</p>\n<p><strong>Perplexity</strong> is <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">e</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8491em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord text mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">loss</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>. A loss of 2.3 nats is a perplexity of 10: the model is as unsure as if it were picking evenly among 10 tokens.</p>\n<h3>In code</h3>\n<pre class=\"code\"><code class=\"language-python\">import torch, torch.nn.functional as F\n\np = torch.tensor([0.5, 0.25, 0.125, 0.125])\nH_bits = -(p * torch.log2(p)).sum()          # 1.75\n\nlogits = torch.tensor([[2.0, 0.75, 0.06]])  # raw scores, cat/dog/bird\ntarget = torch.tensor([0])                  # cat\nF.cross_entropy(logits, target)             # softmax then -ln q[cat]\n</code></pre>\n<p><code>F.cross_entropy</code> takes <strong>logits</strong>, not probabilities. It applies softmax for you, in a numerically safe way.</p>\n<h3>Common mistakes</h3>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Passing probabilities to <code>F.cross_entropy</code>.</strong> It softmaxes again, which squashes everything and gives a wrong, flat loss.</li>\n<li><strong>Comparing losses in bits with losses in nats.</strong> Check the log base before comparing numbers from two papers.</li>\n<li><strong>Thinking low entropy means a good model.</strong> A model that is always 100% sure has zero entropy, and terrible cross-entropy whenever it is wrong.</li>\n<li><strong>Taking <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8889em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">lo<span style=\"margin-right:0.0139em;\">g</span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span></span>.</strong> Add a tiny epsilon or work with logits and <code>log_softmax</code>.</li>\n</ul>\n<h3>Try it yourself</h3>\n<ol>\n<li>What is the entropy of a fair 8-sided die, in bits?</li>\n<li>A model gives the correct token probability 0.25. What is the loss in nats and the perplexity?</li>\n<li>Why can entropy never be negative?</li>\n</ol>\n<details><summary>Answers</summary><ol>\n<li><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" class=\"deeper\"><summary>Go deeper: the original, more advanced notes (3 sections)</summary><section class=\"art\"><h2>Entropy, cross-entropy and KL divergence</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Chris Olah, Visual Information Theory · Goodfellow et al. §3.13 · MML §6.6 · Murphy PML1 ch. 6 · John Schulman, Approximating KL Divergence · Fanout 01.11, 01.12, 02.02</p><p>Olah&#39;s essay builds these from code lengths. If an event has probability <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span>, the optimal code spends <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\"><span class=\"mop\">lo<span style=\"margin-right:0.0139em;\">g</span></span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.207em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.4559em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">2</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.2441em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">1/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> bits on it. Rare events get long codes; Morse code gives &quot;E&quot;, the most common English letter, a single dot.</p>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Self-information</strong> <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" Right: the same logits passed through softmax at three temperatures. Low temperature concentrates probability (low entropy); high temperature spreads it out.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>Since <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0813em;\">H</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> does not depend on the model <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span>, minimising cross-entropy against the data distribution is minimising KL to it. A language model with loss 2.0 nats per token assigns the true next token probability <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">e</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">−</span><span class=\"mord mtight\">2</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0.135</span></span></span></span> on (geometric) average, and its <strong>perplexity</strong> <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">e</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.8141em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">2.0</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">7.4</span></span></span></span> is the effective number of choices it is torn between.</p>\n<p>A useful sanity check: at initialisation a model that predicts uniformly over a vocabulary of <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">V</span></span></span></span> tokens has loss <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">ln</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">V</span></span></span></span>. For GPT-2&#39;s vocabulary of 50,257 tokens that is about 10.82. If your first-step loss is far from this, something is wrong.</p>\n<p><strong>Mutual information</strong> <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0785em;\">I</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0785em;\">X</span><span class=\"mpunct\">;</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">Y</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.2em;vertical-align:-0.35em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">D</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.3283em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.55em;margin-left:-0.0278em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord text mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">KL</span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mopen\"><span class=\"delimsizing size1\">(</span></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mpunct\">,</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">∥</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mclose\"><span class=\"delimsizing size1\">)</span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0813em;\">H</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0785em;\">X</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0813em;\">H</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0785em;\">X</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">∣</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\">Y</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> measures how much knowing one variable reduces uncertainty about the other. It is zero exactly when they are independent.</p>\n<h3>Forward vs reverse KL</h3>\n<p>KL is not symmetric, and Goodfellow et al. §3.13 stress that the direction you choose changes the answer.</p>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Forward KL</strong>, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">D</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.3283em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.55em;margin-left:-0.0278em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord text mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">KL</span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mord\">∥</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>, averages over <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span>. Wherever <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7335em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">&gt;</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span></span> and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6776em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">≈</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span></span> the cost explodes, so <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> must cover every mode of <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span>. It is <strong>mass-covering</strong>. Maximum likelihood minimises this direction.</li>\n<li><strong>Reverse KL</strong>, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">D</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.3283em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.55em;margin-left:-0.0278em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord text mtight\"><span class=\"mord mtight\">KL</span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.15em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span><span class=\"mord\">∥</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>, averages over <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span>. It only cares about places <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> puts mass, so <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> can sit on one mode and ignore the rest. It is <strong>mode-seeking</strong>. Variational inference and the KL penalty in RLHF use this direction.</li>\n</ul>\n<figure class=\"dg fig\"><div class=\"fig-scroll\"><svg xmlns:xlink=\"http://www.w3.org/1999/xlink\" viewBox=\"0 0 class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> to a two-component mixture <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span> by brute-force search over its mean and width. Forward KL blurs across both modes; reverse KL locks onto one. This reproduces the experiment in Goodfellow et al. Figure 3.6.</figcaption></figure>\n<h3>Estimating KL from samples</h3>\n<p>In RL for LLMs you cannot sum over all sequences, so KL is estimated from samples <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">∼</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span></span></span></span> with ratio <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0278em;\">r</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">=</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mord\">/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">q</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>. You will see it again in Lesson 07.05.</p>\n<h3>Sampling: temperature, top-k and top-p</h3>\n<p>A language model produces a categorical distribution, and decoding chooses how to sample from it. <strong>Temperature</strong> <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">T</span></span></span></span> divides the logits: <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7224em;vertical-align:-0.0391em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">T</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">&lt;</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1</span></span></span></span> sharpens toward the argmax (lower entropy), <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7224em;vertical-align:-0.0391em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">T</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">&gt;</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">1</span></span></span></span> flattens, and <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">T</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span><span class=\"mrel\">→</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2778em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6444em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">0</span></span></span></span> is greedy decoding. <strong>Top-k</strong> keeps the <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.6944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0315em;\">k</span></span></span></span> most likely tokens and renormalises. <strong>Top-p</strong> (nucleus) keeps the smallest set whose cumulative probability reaches <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span>, so it adapts: few tokens when the model is confident, many when it is not. Exercise 3 implements all three and their edge cases (ties, <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span></span></span></span> larger than any prefix).</p>\n</section><section class=\"art extra\"><div class=\"xlab\">Extra · goes beyond the Fanout lesson</div><h2>Probability you will use every day</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> Harvard Stat 110 lectures 1-24 · MML §6.1-6.4 · Goodfellow et al. §3.1-3.11 · Murphy PML1 ch. 2-3</p><p>Stat 110 builds probability from counting up. For ML you need a working toolkit more than the full axiomatics, so here it is, organised the way Goodfellow et al. chapter 3 organise it.</p>\n<h3>Random variables and distributions</h3>\n<p>A random variable assigns a number to each outcome. A <strong>discrete</strong> variable has a probability mass function <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.1389em;\">P</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> that sums to 1. A <strong>continuous</strong> variable has a density <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">p</span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span><span The base rate dominates. This is the most common probabilistic mistake in evaluating classifiers, and the reason precision and recall are reported separately.</p>\n<p>Stat 110&#39;s advice is to condition on whatever you wish you knew. of all distributions with a given variance it has the <strong>maximum entropy</strong>, so it builds in the fewest assumptions. 01.03&#39;s SVD picture.</p>\n<h3>The central limit theorem</h3>\n<p>The mean of many independent variables with finite variance is approximately Gaussian, whatever the original distribution. That is why you can put a <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.7667em;vertical-align:-0.0833em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">±</span><span class=\"mord\">1.96</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord text\"><span class=\"mord\">SE</span></span></span></span></span> interval on an accuracy averaged over 1,000 eval items. It gets unreliable when <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">n</span></span></span></span> is small or the accuracy is near 0 or 1; then use an exact binomial interval (Clopper-Pearson) or the Wilson interval.</p>\n</section><section class=\"art extra\"><div class=\"xlab\">Extra · goes beyond the Fanout lesson</div><h2>Maximum likelihood: where loss functions come from</h2><p class=\"cite\"><span class=\"lab\">Based on</span> MML §8.3 · Goodfellow et al. §5.5, §6.2.1 · Murphy PML1 §4.2 · Stat 110</p><p>Most loss functions in deep learning are negative log-likelihoods of a simple probabilistic model. If you can name the model, you understand the loss.</p>\n<p><strong>Maximum likelihood estimation</strong> picks parameters that make the observed data most probable. With i.i.d. examples,</p>\n<div class=\"math-block\"><span class=\"katex-display\"><span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span MSE is the Gaussian assumption in disguise, which is why it is sensitive to outliers: Gaussian tails are thin, so a large residual is treated as extremely unlikely and penalised heavily. Assume Laplace noise instead and the NLL becomes <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" </div><figcaption>Left: Gaussian and Laplace densities. Right: their negative log-likelihoods, which are the squared-error and absolute-error losses. A residual of 4 costs 8 under the Gaussian and 4 under the Laplace.</figcaption></figure>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §6.2.1.2 make a further point: the minimiser of expected squared error is the conditional <strong>mean</strong> of <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.625em;vertical-align:-0.1944em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span></span></span></span> given <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">x</span></span></span></span>, and the minimiser of absolute error is the conditional <strong>median</strong>. Choosing the loss chooses which statistic your model predicts.</p>\n<h3>Categorical outputs give cross-entropy</h3>\n<p>For classification the model outputs <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" The NLL is <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.9805em;vertical-align:-0.2861em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mop\">lo<span style=\"margin-right:0.0139em;\">g</span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">π</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.1514em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.55em;margin-left:-0.0359em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal mtight\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.2861em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>, which is cross-entropy against a one-hot target. Binary cross-entropy is the Bernoulli case with a sigmoid. Language-model pretraining loss is this, averaged over every token position.</p>\n<p>Goodfellow et al. §6.2.2 explain why this pairing works so well for learning. <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord text\"><span class=\"mord\">onehot</span></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span>. That gradient is large when the model is confidently wrong. Squared error on probabilities would saturate instead: when the softmax output is near 0 or 1, its derivative is tiny, and the model stops learning from its worst mistakes.</p>\n<h3>Reading a loss as a model</h3>\n<ul>\n<li><strong>Label smoothing</strong> with <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϵ</span></span></span></span> replaces the one-hot target with <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">1</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϵ</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.1667em;\"></span><span class=\"mord text\"><span class=\"mord\">onehot</span></span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">+</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϵ</span><span class=\"mord\">/</span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0715em;\">K</span></span></span></span>. It is cross-entropy against that softened target, which works out to <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">1</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϵ</span><span class=\"mclose\">)</span></span></span></span> times the usual loss plus <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:0.4306em;\"></span><span class=\"mord mathnormal\">ϵ</span></span></span></span> times the cross-entropy against the uniform distribution. The second term penalises overconfident predictions.</li>\n<li><strong>Focal loss</strong> multiplies cross-entropy by <span class=\"katex\"><span class=\"katex-html\" aria-hidden=\"true\"><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1em;vertical-align:-0.25em;\"></span><span class=\"mopen\">(</span><span class=\"mord\">1</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span><span class=\"mbin\">−</span><span class=\"mspace\" style=\"margin-right:0.2222em;\"></span></span><span class=\"katex-base\"><span class=\"katex-strut\" style=\"height:1.0361em;vertical-align:-0.2861em;\"></span><span class=\"mord\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">π</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t vlist-t2\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.1514em;\"><span style=\"top:-2.55em;margin-left:-0.0359em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal mtight\" style=\"margin-right:0.0359em;\">y</span></span></span></span><span class=\"vlist-s\">​</span></span><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.2861em;\"><span></span></span></span></span></span></span><span class=\"mclose\"><span class=\"mclose\">)</span><span class=\"msupsub\"><span class=\"vlist-t\"><span class=\"vlist-r\"><span class=\"vlist\" style=\"height:0.6644em;\"><span style=\"top:-3.063em;margin-right:0.05em;\"><span class=\"pstrut\" style=\"height:2.7em;\"></span><span class=\"katex-sizing reset-size6 size3 mtight\"><span class=\"mord mathnormal mtight\" style=\"margin-right:0.0556em;\">γ</span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span></span>, down-weighting easy examples. is L2 weight decay. A Laplace prior gives L1. Regularisation is a prior belief that weights are small.</p>\n</section></details>"
lead: "Every language model is trained by minimising <strong>cross-entropy</strong>. When a paper reports &quot;perplexity&quot; or &quot;bits per byte&quot;, that is entropy too. This lesson builds the idea from one question: how surprised should you be?"
